I’ll just be sitting there, usually in the cab of my truck, looking at all the empty containers and wrappers piled up, and I just feel completely disgusted with myself. I mean, I feel like I’m not the steady, in-control man I’ve always tried to be. \n\nAnd you really gotta be in control when you're hauling eighty-thousand pounds down the interstate, you know? I've been driving routes for almost thirty years. I actually got my associate's degree a long time ago thinking I'd go into logistics, maybe sit in an air-conditioned office somewhere. My buddy Tom did that, ended up with a nice desk job over in Toledo, but he absolutely hates it. Says the fluorescent lights give him terrible migraines, and his boss is a real piece of work. I always preferred the open road, just me and the radio, seeing the country... but anyway, yeah. I just sit there staring at the empty food containers and I don't even recognize the guy who did it.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you've done, before feeling disgusted, that's causing you distress?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's the eating. Like, some evenings I'll be at home, right? And I'll heat up what I swear is just one bowl of pozole. My sister makes it, she brings over these big batches. She’s always been the cook in the family, ever since our mom passed. Mom used to make this amazing green chili, and she’d simmer it all day. You could smell it from down the street. My sister tries, but it’s never quite the same, you know? She uses too much cumin, I think. Anyway, I'll start with just one bowl of the pozole, and before I know it, I’ve gone through the whole pot. And then a couple of tamales, a whole bag of chips, and whatever other leftovers are sitting in the fridge. It's like I'm feeding a whole crew instead of just myself. And it's not even like I'm tasting it after a while. I just... I just keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed or upset, or can it happen at any time?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I haven't really paid attention to whether I'm upset or if it just... happens whenever. I mean, stress is just kind of a constant, right? Especially on the road. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was taking a load down through Atlanta, and the traffic there is just an absolute nightmare. They’ve been doing construction on I-285 for what feels like a decade now. You’d think with all the highway taxes they collect, they could finish a simple repaving job on schedule. I was talking to my dispatcher, Jerry, about it on the radio—Jerry’s a decent guy, but he’s always breathing down your neck about delivery windows—and he was telling me his brother-in-law runs a paving company down in Georgia. Apparently, the cost of asphalt has gone through the roof lately. Something about the supply chains being all messed up still. It's crazy how everything is connected like that. \n\nBut... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about being stressed out when I eat. Yeah, I don't really know. I just haven't noticed if there's a pattern to it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating this way helps you feel better when you're dealing with stressful situations like the traffic in Atlanta?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it makes me feel better. I guess I haven't thought about it like that. I mean, when I'm stuck in that Atlanta traffic, the only thing that really makes me feel better is finally seeing the city limits in my rearview mirror. \n\nYou know, speaking of rearview mirrors, I had to replace the passenger side one on my rig a couple of weeks ago. Some guy in a rented box truck—you know the ones, where they just let anybody with a valid license drive a massive vehicle with zero training—he clipped me at a truck stop outside of Nashville. Didn't even stop. I had to call it in, wait for the company mechanic to authorize a replacement... it was a whole ordeal. And those mirrors aren't cheap, let me tell you. They've got the heating elements in them for the winter, the motorized adjustment... it's a piece of engineering. My buddy Tom, the one in Toledo, he was telling me about how they're putting cameras instead of mirrors on some of the new European trucks. Says it cuts down on aerodynamic drag. But I don't know, I just don't trust a screen the way I trust a piece of glass, you know? What if the camera glitches out when you're merging? \n\nBut, uh... I don't know if the eating helps with the stress. I just haven't really noticed if it does or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat this way more often when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: More often? I... I really couldn't say for sure if the frequency goes up. Like I said, the stress is just sort of always there in the background. \n\nYou know what really gets the blood pressure going, though? Those random DOT inspections. Man, you'll be rolling through Ohio, just trying to make good time, and the signs light up telling you to pull into the weigh station. You just pray they wave you through the bypass lane, but last month, this officer—young guy, probably fresh out of training and looking to prove something—decides he wants to do a full Level 1 inspection on my rig. Crawling under the trailer, checking the pushrod travel on the air brakes, going through my electronic logs with a fine-tooth comb. I keep my truck in top shape, always have, but they can always find *something* to write up if they look hard enough, you know? A marker light flickering, a mudflap half an inch too high. It took almost two hours, and by the time he finally handed me the paperwork, my whole schedule for the day was completely shot. I had to call the receiver, reschedule the dock time... it's a whole domino effect. The supply chain doesn't wait for anybody. \n\nBut, uh... gosh, I'm sorry. Did you ask if I eat more often when stuff like that happens? I honestly haven't kept track. I just don't know if there's a connection there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat this way as a way to deal with stress?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I really don't know. I haven't thought about it as a way to deal with things. It's just... I just eat. \n\nDealing with stress, though... my buddy Carl, he used to drive flatbeds out of Texas, he had this whole system for dealing with stress. He'd listen to these audiobooks on tape—well, CDs back then, I guess it's all on your phone now. But he’d listen to these crazy long biographies of presidents. Said it put his own problems in perspective. Like, he'd be stuck in a snowstorm in Wyoming, and he'd be listening to Abraham Lincoln dealing with the Civil War, and suddenly being delayed a day didn't seem so bad. Carl actually retired last year. Bought a little piece of land up in the panhandle, got a couple of horses. He always said he wanted to get away from the highways, and he finally did it. Good for him, you know? It takes a toll on your body, sitting in that seat for thirty years. Your lower back just goes. I've got this special lumbar support cushion I bought at a truck stop in Iowa, memory foam, cost me sixty bucks, but it's worth every penny. \n\nBut, uh... dealing with stress by eating? I honestly couldn't tell you. I just haven't noticed if that's why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, doc, I just don't know. Calming down? Feeling better? It's hard to say if the food actually does that. I've never really paid attention to whether I feel calmer after. \n\nSpeaking of calming down, though, you ever try to calm down a dispatcher when a load of refrigerated produce gets held up? Now that's a situation. I had this load of strawberries coming out of California a few years back. The reefer unit—the cooling system on the trailer—started acting up somewhere in New Mexico. The temperature gauge kept fluctuating, and with berries, you've got a really narrow window before they turn to mush. I'm on the phone with dispatch, and this guy, Kevin, he's practically hyperventilating on the other end of the line. \"Get it to a shop! Don't let the load spoil!\" Like I'm doing it on purpose, right? I had to pull into this tiny little repair place in Gallup. The mechanic there, old guy named Hector, he took one look at it, tapped a valve with a wrench, and the compressor kicked right back on. Charged me twenty bucks. Kevin was so relieved he actually authorized a steak dinner on the company card for that night. Best ribeye I ever had, right there at a diner off I-40. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... does eating calm me down? I really couldn't give you a straight answer on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: More when I'm anxious? I... I really don't think I could say. I just haven't kept track of it like that. I don't know if there's a pattern with the anxiety.\n\nYou know what makes me anxious, though? Driving through Atlanta during rush hour. I swear, they must hand out driver's licenses in cereal boxes down there. It's like a free-for-all. You've got these little sports cars darting in and out of the blind spots on a 70-foot rig, cutting across three lanes of traffic just to make an exit at the last possible second. My buddy Steve, he actually had a guy clip his front bumper doing that down near Macon. The guy spun out, went into the median, whole big mess. Steve had a dashcam running, thank God, or the insurance company would've tried to pin it on him. They always try to blame the trucker. I bought one of those dashcams myself right after that. Got it off Amazon, dual-lens, records the cab and the road. The memory card loop-records, so you don't even have to mess with it unless something happens. Peace of mind, you know? \n\nBut, uh... eating when I'm anxious? I just... I really don't know if I eat more then. I couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with stressful situations?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know, doc. Coping with stress? I just haven't really put two and two together on that. I don't know if it helps me cope or not.\n\nSpeaking of coping with situations, though... you ever try to find a parking spot at a rest stop on the I-95 corridor after 9 PM? Now that's stressful. You're running out of hours on your electronic log—the ELD, you know, it tracks every minute you're driving, and if you go over your 11 hours, you get a violation. So you're sweating, looking at the clock ticking down, pulling into these service plazas in Maryland or Delaware, and it's just wall-to-wall trucks. Guys are parked on the shoulders, on the off-ramps, making up spots that aren't even spots. I remember this one night, it was pouring rain, I had maybe fifteen minutes left on my clock, and I finally squeezed into this dirt lot behind a diner in Virginia. Woke up the next morning and my drive tires were sunk three inches deep in mud. Took me an hour of rocking the rig back and forth just to get out of there. Ruined a perfectly good pair of work boots, too. I had to buy a new pair at a Tractor Supply in Richmond. Good boots, though, steel-toed, waterproof. You need good arch support when you're jumping in and out of the cab all day. \n\nBut... using food to cope? I honestly don't know. I just haven't noticed if that's what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something you do to try and make yourself feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... well, making myself feel better? I don't think I can give you a real answer on that either. I just don't know if that's why I do it. I haven't really thought about it that way.\n\nYou want to talk about trying to make yourself feel better, though, you should try sleeping in a sleeper berth when it's 10 degrees outside and the APU goes down. The auxiliary power unit, it's what keeps the heat running without idling the main engine. I was up in North Dakota, near Fargo, middle of January. Woke up at 2 AM and I could see my breath in the cab. The APU had just quit. Turns out the fuel line to it had gelled up because of the cold. I had to bundle up in every blanket I had, plus my heavy winter coat, just shivering until the sun came up and I could get the main engine started to warm the place up. I carry a bottle of diesel 911 now, anti-gel additive, you know? Pour a little in the tanks when it gets that cold. Saves you a lot of grief. I get it by the gallon at the truck stops. \n\nBut, uh... eating to feel better? I really don't know, doc. I just haven't paid enough attention to tell you yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something that happens when you're trying to calm down after a stressful event?\n\nPatient: Calming down? I... I really couldn't say for sure. I don't know if I reach for food specifically to calm down. I just haven't connected those dots, honestly.\n\nBut if you want to talk about trying to calm down after something stressful... man, let me tell you about this blowout I had on I-80 out in Nebraska last summer. I'm hauling 40,000 pounds of frozen chicken, right? Just cruising along, maybe 65 miles an hour, and suddenly—BAM. Sounded like a shotgun went off right under my seat. My front right steer tire just shredded. The whole rig jerked hard toward the ditch. I had to wrestle that steering wheel with everything I had just to keep it on the pavement and ease it onto the shoulder without flipping the trailer. My heart was beating so hard I thought it was going to crack my ribs. I sat there on the side of the highway for a good twenty minutes, hands just shaking on the wheel, trying to get my breathing back to normal. Took road service three hours to get out there with a new tire. Cost the company a fortune, but hey, at least I didn't roll it. You really appreciate a good heavy-duty jack after watching a guy lift a fully loaded rig on the side of a busy interstate. \n\nBut as for the eating to calm down thing... I don't know, doc. I really just haven't noticed if that's what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this way if you weren't feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't stressed? I... I honestly couldn't tell you, doc. I don't even know what it looks like to not be stressed out, so I really don't know if my eating would change or not. I just haven't experienced it to know. \n\nI mean, speaking of not being stressed, the only time I ever really feel relaxed out on the road is when I'm bobtailing. That's when you're just driving the tractor, no trailer attached. It's a whole different ride. The suspension is a little stiff because it's meant to hold up tens of thousands of pounds, but man, the truck feels like a sports car compared to hauling a fully loaded 53-foot reefer. You can actually accelerate up a hill. But even then, your relaxation doesn't last long because you always gotta worry about the DOT. \n\nDepartment of Transportation, you know? You roll into a weigh station and they hit the bypass light and pull you around back for a Level 1 inspection. They're checking your slack adjusters, your airlines, crawling all under the rig on a creeper. I had this one inspector in Ohio last spring, guy spent forty-five minutes checking every single lug nut. Wrote me up because one of my amber clearance lights on the cab roof was flickering. Just flickering, not even completely burnt out! Cost the company a fine and hit my safety score. You gotta carry spare bulbs for every single socket on that truck, I swear. I keep a whole plastic tackle box full of fuses, headlights, and marker bulbs in my side box now. \n\nBut, uh... what were we saying? Eating when I'm not stressed? Yeah, I really don't have an answer for you on that. I just don't know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just don't know.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, if I found other ways to deal with stress? I really don't know. I can't say for sure if it would make a difference or not. I just haven't really tried anything else, so I couldn't tell you if I'd eat less.

But talking about dealing with stress... you know what really helps when you're out on the road? A good CB radio. A lot of the younger guys don't even use them anymore, they just rely on their phones and GPS, but let me tell you, a CB is a lifesaver. I run a Cobra 29 LX, peaked and tuned, with dual fiberglass whips mounted on the mirrors. You get out in Wyoming or Montana, where the cell service just drops off the face of the earth, and that radio is your only connection to the world. A few months ago, I was heading west on I-90, just past Billings, and the snow was coming down so thick you couldn't see the hood ornament. Total whiteout. But I had the radio on channel 19, and I hear this guy, 'Snowman,' hollering that there's a pileup about two miles ahead, right around a blind curve. Four four-wheelers and a flatbed all tangled up. If I hadn't had that radio, I would have plowed right into them. I was able to gear down, hit the jake brake—the engine retarder, you know, saves your service brakes from burning up on ice—and ease it onto the shoulder before I even saw the taillights. Now I always make sure my SWR meter is calibrated so my signal gets out strong. You gotta check your coax cables every few months too, make sure they aren't pinching in the door frame. 

But, uh... eating less if I wasn't stressed? Yeah, I really don't know, doc. I just don't have a good answer for you on that one.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc... it's kind of hard to say out loud. But what brings me in is... it's what happens after I do it. I’ll just be sitting there, usually in the cab of my truck, looking at all the empty containers and wrappers piled up, and I just feel completely disgusted with myself. I mean, I feel like I’m not the steady, in-control man I’ve always tried to be. \n\nAnd you really gotta be in control when you're hauling eighty-thousand pounds down the interstate, you know? I've been driving routes for almost thirty years. I actually got my associate's degree a long time ago thinking I'd go into logistics, maybe sit in an air-conditioned office somewhere. My buddy Tom did that, ended up with a nice desk job over in Toledo, but he absolutely hates it. Says the fluorescent lights give him terrible migraines, and his boss is a real piece of work. I always preferred the open road, just me and the radio, seeing the country... but anyway, yeah. Like, some evenings I'll be at home, right? And I'll heat up what I swear is just one bowl of pozole. My sister makes it, she brings over these big batches. She’s always been the cook in the family, ever since our mom passed. Mom used to make this amazing green chili, and she’d simmer it all day. You could smell it from down the street. My sister tries, but it’s never quite the same, you know? She uses too much cumin, I think. Anyway, I'll start with just one bowl of the pozole, and before I know it, I’ve gone through the whole pot. Especially on the road. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was taking a load down through Atlanta, and the traffic there is just an absolute nightmare. They’ve been doing construction on I-285 for what feels like a decade now. You’d think with all the highway taxes they collect, they could finish a simple repaving job on schedule. I was talking to my dispatcher, Jerry, about it on the radio—Jerry’s a decent guy, but he’s always breathing down your neck about delivery windows—and he was telling me his brother-in-law runs a paving company down in Georgia. Apparently, the cost of asphalt has gone through the roof lately. Something about the supply chains being all messed up still. It's crazy how everything is connected like that. \n\nBut... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about being stressed out when I eat. Yeah, I don't really know. I just haven't noticed if there's a pattern to it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating this way helps you feel better when you're dealing with stressful situations like the traffic in Atlanta?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it makes me feel better. Some guy in a rented box truck—you know the ones, where they just let anybody with a valid license drive a massive vehicle with zero training—he clipped me at a truck stop outside of Nashville. Didn't even stop. I had to call it in, wait for the company mechanic to authorize a replacement... it was a whole ordeal. And those mirrors aren't cheap, let me tell you. They've got the heating elements in them for the winter, the motorized adjustment... it's a piece of engineering. My buddy Tom, the one in Toledo, he was telling me about how they're putting cameras instead of mirrors on some of the new European trucks. Says it cuts down on aerodynamic drag. I don't know if the eating helps with the stress. I just haven't really noticed if it does or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat this way more often when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: More often? I... I really couldn't say for sure if the frequency goes up. Like I said, the stress is just sort of always there in the background. \n\nYou know what really gets the blood pressure going, though? Those random DOT inspections. Man, you'll be rolling through Ohio, just trying to make good time, and the signs light up telling you to pull into the weigh station. You just pray they wave you through the bypass lane, but last month, this officer—young guy, probably fresh out of training and looking to prove something—decides he wants to do a full Level 1 inspection on my rig. Crawling under the trailer, checking the pushrod travel on the air brakes, going through my electronic logs with a fine-tooth comb. I keep my truck in top shape, always have, but they can always find *something* to write up if they look hard enough, you know? A marker light flickering, a mudflap half an inch too high. It took almost two hours, and by the time he finally handed me the paperwork, my whole schedule for the day was completely shot. I had to call the receiver, reschedule the dock time... it's a whole domino effect. The supply chain doesn't wait for anybody. \n\nBut, uh... gosh, I'm sorry. Did you ask if I eat more often when stuff like that happens? I honestly haven't kept track. I just don't know if there's a connection there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat this way as a way to deal with stress?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I really don't know. I haven't thought about it as a way to deal with things. It's just... I just eat. \n\nDealing with stress, though... my buddy Carl, he used to drive flatbeds out of Texas, he had this whole system for dealing with stress. Said it put his own problems in perspective. Like, he'd be stuck in a snowstorm in Wyoming, and he'd be listening to Abraham Lincoln dealing with the Civil War, and suddenly being delayed a day didn't seem so bad. Carl actually retired last year. Bought a little piece of land up in the panhandle, got a couple of horses. He always said he wanted to get away from the highways, and he finally did it. Good for him, you know? It takes a toll on your body, sitting in that seat for thirty years. Your lower back just goes. I've got this special lumbar support cushion I bought at a truck stop in Iowa, memory foam, cost me sixty bucks, but it's worth every penny. \n\nBut, uh... dealing with stress by eating? I honestly couldn't tell you. I just haven't noticed if that's why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, doc, I just don't know. Calming down? Feeling better? It's hard to say if the food actually does that. I've never really paid attention to whether I feel calmer after. \n\nSpeaking of calming down, though, you ever try to calm down a dispatcher when a load of refrigerated produce gets held up? Now that's a situation. I had this load of strawberries coming out of California a few years back. The reefer unit—the cooling system on the trailer—started acting up somewhere in New Mexico. The temperature gauge kept fluctuating, and with berries, you've got a really narrow window before they turn to mush. I'm on the phone with dispatch, and this guy, Kevin, he's practically hyperventilating on the other end of the line. \"Get it to a shop! Don't let the load spoil!\" Like I'm doing it on purpose, right? I had to pull into this tiny little repair place in Gallup. The mechanic there, old guy named Hector, he took one look at it, tapped a valve with a wrench, and the compressor kicked right back on. Charged me twenty bucks. Kevin was so relieved he actually authorized a steak dinner on the company card for that night. Best ribeye I ever had, right there at a diner off I-40. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... does eating calm me down? I really couldn't give you a straight answer on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: More when I'm anxious? I... I really don't think I could say. I just haven't kept track of it like that. I don't know if there's a pattern with the anxiety.\n\nYou know what makes me anxious, though? Driving through Atlanta during rush hour. I swear, they must hand out driver's licenses in cereal boxes down there. It's like a free-for-all. You've got these little sports cars darting in and out of the blind spots on a 70-foot rig, cutting across three lanes of traffic just to make an exit at the last possible second. My buddy Steve, he actually had a guy clip his front bumper doing that down near Macon. The guy spun out, went into the median, whole big mess. Steve had a dashcam running, thank God, or the insurance company would've tried to pin it on him. They always try to blame the trucker. I bought one of those dashcams myself right after that. Got it off Amazon, dual-lens, records the cab and the road. The memory card loop-records, so you don't even have to mess with it unless something happens. Peace of mind, you know? \n\nBut, uh... eating when I'm anxious? I just... I really don't know if I eat more then. I couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with stressful situations?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know, doc. Coping with stress? I just haven't really put two and two together on that. I don't know if it helps me cope or not.\n\nSpeaking of coping with situations, though... you ever try to find a parking spot at a rest stop on the I-95 corridor after 9 PM? Now that's stressful. You're running out of hours on your electronic log—the ELD, you know, it tracks every minute you're driving, and if you go over your 11 hours, you get a violation. So you're sweating, looking at the clock ticking down, pulling into these service plazas in Maryland or Delaware, and it's just wall-to-wall trucks. Guys are parked on the shoulders, on the off-ramps, making up spots that aren't even spots. I remember this one night, it was pouring rain, I had maybe fifteen minutes left on my clock, and I finally squeezed into this dirt lot behind a diner in Virginia. Woke up the next morning and my drive tires were sunk three inches deep in mud. Took me an hour of rocking the rig back and forth just to get out of there. Ruined a perfectly good pair of work boots, too. I had to buy a new pair at a Tractor Supply in Richmond. Good boots, though, steel-toed, waterproof. You need good arch support when you're jumping in and out of the cab all day. \n\nBut... using food to cope? I honestly don't know. I just haven't noticed if that's what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something you do to try and make yourself feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... well, making myself feel better? I don't think I can give you a real answer on that either. I just don't know if that's why I do it. I haven't really thought about it that way.\n\nYou want to talk about trying to make yourself feel better, though, you should try sleeping in a sleeper berth when it's 10 degrees outside and the APU goes down. The auxiliary power unit, it's what keeps the heat running without idling the main engine. I was up in North Dakota, near Fargo, middle of January. Woke up at 2 AM and I could see my breath in the cab. The APU had just quit. Turns out the fuel line to it had gelled up because of the cold. I had to bundle up in every blanket I had, plus my heavy winter coat, just shivering until the sun came up and I could get the main engine started to warm the place up. I carry a bottle of diesel 911 now, anti-gel additive, you know? Pour a little in the tanks when it gets that cold. Saves you a lot of grief. I get it by the gallon at the truck stops. \n\nBut, uh... eating to feel better? I really don't know, doc. I just haven't paid enough attention to tell you yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something that happens when you're trying to calm down after a stressful event?\n\nPatient: Calming down? I... I really couldn't say for sure. I don't know if I reach for food specifically to calm down. I just haven't connected those dots, honestly.\n\nBut if you want to talk about trying to calm down after something stressful... man, let me tell you about this blowout I had on I-80 out in Nebraska last summer. I'm hauling 40,000 pounds of frozen chicken, right? Just cruising along, maybe 65 miles an hour, and suddenly—BAM. Sounded like a shotgun went off right under my seat. My front right steer tire just shredded. The whole rig jerked hard toward the ditch. I had to wrestle that steering wheel with everything I had just to keep it on the pavement and ease it onto the shoulder without flipping the trailer. My heart was beating so hard I thought it was going to crack my ribs. I sat there on the side of the highway for a good twenty minutes, hands just shaking on the wheel, trying to get my breathing back to normal. Took road service three hours to get out there with a new tire. Cost the company a fortune, but hey, at least I didn't roll it. You really appreciate a good heavy-duty jack after watching a guy lift a fully loaded rig on the side of a busy interstate. \n\nBut as for the eating to calm down thing... I don't know, doc. I really just haven't noticed if that's what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this way if you weren't feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't stressed? I... I honestly couldn't tell you, doc. I don't even know what it looks like to not be stressed out, so I really don't know if my eating would change or not. I just haven't experienced it to know. \n\nI mean, speaking of not being stressed, the only time I ever really feel relaxed out on the road is when I'm bobtailing. That's when you're just driving the tractor, no trailer attached. It's a whole different ride. The suspension is a little stiff because it's meant to hold up tens of thousands of pounds, but man, the truck feels like a sports car compared to hauling a fully loaded 53-foot reefer. You can actually accelerate up a hill. But even then, your relaxation doesn't last long because you always gotta worry about the DOT. \n\nDepartment of Transportation, you know? You roll into a weigh station and they hit the bypass light and pull you around back for a Level 1 inspection. They're checking your slack adjusters, your airlines, crawling all under the rig on a creeper. I had this one inspector in Ohio last spring, guy spent forty-five minutes checking every single lug nut. Wrote me up because one of my amber clearance lights on the cab roof was flickering. Just flickering, not even completely burnt out! Cost the company a fine and hit my safety score. You gotta carry spare bulbs for every single socket on that truck, I swear. I keep a whole plastic tackle box full of fuses, headlights, and marker bulbs in my side box now. \n\nBut, uh... what were we saying? Eating when I'm not stressed? Yeah, I really don't have an answer for you on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\nPatient: I... well, if I found other ways to deal with stress? I really don't know. I can't say for sure if it would make a difference or not. I just haven't really tried anything else, so I couldn't tell you if I'd eat less.\n\nBut talking about dealing with stress... you know what really helps when you're out on the road? A good CB radio. A lot of the younger guys don't even use them anymore, they just rely on their phones and GPS, but let me tell you, a CB is a lifesaver. I was able to gear down, hit the jake brake—the engine retarder, you know, saves your service brakes from burning up on ice—and ease it onto the shoulder before I even saw the taillights. Now I always make sure my SWR meter is calibrated so my signal gets out strong. I just haven't noticed if that's why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, doc, I just don't know. Calming down? Feeling better? It's hard to say if the food actually does that. I've never really paid attention to whether I feel calmer after. \n\nSpeaking of calming down, though, you ever try to calm down a dispatcher when a load of refrigerated produce gets held up? Now that's a situation. I had this load of strawberries coming out of California a few years back. The reefer unit—the cooling system on the trailer—started acting up somewhere in New Mexico. The temperature gauge kept fluctuating, and with berries, you've got a really narrow window before they turn to mush. I'm on the phone with dispatch, and this guy, Kevin, he's practically hyperventilating on the other end of the line. \"Get it to a shop! Don't let the load spoil!\" Like I'm doing it on purpose, right? I had to pull into this tiny little repair place in Gallup. The mechanic there, old guy named Hector, he took one look at it, tapped a valve with a wrench, and the compressor kicked right back on. Charged me twenty bucks. Kevin was so relieved he actually authorized a steak dinner on the company card for that night. Best ribeye I ever had, right there at a diner off I-40. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... does eating calm me down? I really couldn't give you a straight answer on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: More when I'm anxious? I... I really don't think I could say. I just haven't kept track of it like that. I don't know if there's a pattern with the anxiety.\n\nYou know what makes me anxious, though? Driving through Atlanta during rush hour. I swear, they must hand out driver's licenses in cereal boxes down there. It's like a free-for-all. You've got these little sports cars darting in and out of the blind spots on a 70-foot rig, cutting across three lanes of traffic just to make an exit at the last possible second. My buddy Steve, he actually had a guy clip his front bumper doing that down near Macon. The guy spun out, went into the median, whole big mess. Steve had a dashcam running, thank God, or the insurance company would've tried to pin it on him. They always try to blame the trucker. I bought one of those dashcams myself right after that. Got it off Amazon, dual-lens, records the cab and the road. The memory card loop-records, so you don't even have to mess with it unless something happens. Peace of mind, you know? \n\nBut, uh... eating when I'm anxious? I just... I really don't know if I eat more then. I couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with stressful situations?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know, doc. Coping with stress? I just haven't really put two and two together on that. I don't know if it helps me cope or not.\n\nSpeaking of coping with situations, though... you ever try to find a parking spot at a rest stop on the I-95 corridor after 9 PM? Now that's stressful. You're running out of hours on your electronic log—the ELD, you know, it tracks every minute you're driving, and if you go over your 11 hours, you get a violation. So you're sweating, looking at the clock ticking down, pulling into these service plazas in Maryland or Delaware, and it's just wall-to-wall trucks. Guys are parked on the shoulders, on the off-ramps, making up spots that aren't even spots. I remember this one night, it was pouring rain, I had maybe fifteen minutes left on my clock, and I finally squeezed into this dirt lot behind a diner in Virginia. Woke up the next morning and my drive tires were sunk three inches deep in mud. Took me an hour of rocking the rig back and forth just to get out of there. Ruined a perfectly good pair of work boots, too. I had to buy a new pair at a Tractor Supply in Richmond. Good boots, though, steel-toed, waterproof. You need good arch support when you're jumping in and out of the cab all day. \n\nBut... using food to cope? I honestly don't know. I just haven't noticed if that's what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something you do to try and make yourself feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... well, making myself feel better? I don't think I can give you a real answer on that either. I just don't know if that's why I do it. I haven't really thought about it that way.\n\nYou want to talk about trying to make yourself feel better, though, you should try sleeping in a sleeper berth when it's 10 degrees outside and the APU goes down. The auxiliary power unit, it's what keeps the heat running without idling the main engine. I was up in North Dakota, near Fargo, middle of January. Woke up at 2 AM and I could see my breath in the cab. The APU had just quit. Turns out the fuel line to it had gelled up because of the cold. I had to bundle up in every blanket I had, plus my heavy winter coat, just shivering until the sun came up and I could get the main engine started to warm the place up. I carry a bottle of diesel 911 now, anti-gel additive, you know? Pour a little in the tanks when it gets that cold. Saves you a lot of grief. I get it by the gallon at the truck stops. \n\nBut, uh... eating to feel better? I really don't know, doc. I just haven't paid enough attention to tell you yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something that happens when you're trying to calm down after a stressful event?\n\nPatient: Calming down? I... I really couldn't say for sure. I don't know if I reach for food specifically to calm down. I just haven't connected those dots, honestly.\n\nBut if you want to talk about trying to calm down after something stressful... man, let me tell you about this blowout I had on I-80 out in Nebraska last summer. I'm hauling 40,000 pounds of frozen chicken, right? Just cruising along, maybe 65 miles an hour, and suddenly—BAM. Sounded like a shotgun went off right under my seat. My front right steer tire just shredded. The whole rig jerked hard toward the ditch. I had to wrestle that steering wheel with everything I had just to keep it on the pavement and ease it onto the shoulder without flipping the trailer. My heart was beating so hard I thought it was going to crack my ribs. I sat there on the side of the highway for a good twenty minutes, hands just shaking on the wheel, trying to get my breathing back to normal. Took road service three hours to get out there with a new tire. Cost the company a fortune, but hey, at least I didn't roll it. You really appreciate a good heavy-duty jack after watching a guy lift a fully loaded rig on the side of a busy interstate. \n\nBut as for the eating to calm down thing... I don't know, doc. I really just haven't noticed if that's what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this way if you weren't feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't stressed? I... I honestly couldn't tell you, doc. I don't even know what it looks like to not be stressed out, so I really don't know if my eating would change or not. I just haven't experienced it to know. \n\nI mean, speaking of not being stressed, the only time I ever really feel relaxed out on the road is when I'm bobtailing. That's when you're just driving the tractor, no trailer attached. It's a whole different ride. The suspension is a little stiff because it's meant to hold up tens of thousands of pounds, but man, the truck feels like a sports car compared to hauling a fully loaded 53-foot reefer. You can actually accelerate up a hill. But even then, your relaxation doesn't last long because you always gotta worry about the DOT. \n\nDepartment of Transportation, you know? You roll into a weigh station and they hit the bypass light and pull you around back for a Level 1 inspection. They're checking your slack adjusters, your airlines, crawling all under the rig on a creeper. I had this one inspector in Ohio last spring, guy spent forty-five minutes checking every single lug nut. Wrote me up because one of my amber clearance lights on the cab roof was flickering. Just flickering, not even completely burnt out! Cost the company a fine and hit my safety score. You gotta carry spare bulbs for every single socket on that truck, I swear. I keep a whole plastic tackle box full of fuses, headlights, and marker bulbs in my side box now. \n\nBut, uh... what were we saying? Eating when I'm not stressed? Yeah, I really don't have an answer for you on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\nPatient: I... well, if I found other ways to deal with stress? I really don't know. I can't say for sure if it would make a difference or not. I just haven't really tried anything else, so I couldn't tell you if I'd eat less.\n\nBut talking about dealing with stress... you know what really helps when you're out on the road? A good CB radio. A lot of the younger guys don't even use them anymore, they just rely on their phones and GPS, but let me tell you, a CB is a lifesaver. I run a Cobra 29 LX, peaked and tuned, with dual fiberglass whips mounted on the mirrors. You get out in Wyoming or Montana, where the cell service just drops off the face of the earth, and that radio is your only connection to the world. A few months ago, I was heading west on I-90, just past Billings, and the snow was coming down so thick you couldn't see the hood ornament. Total whiteout. But I had the radio on channel 19, and I hear this guy, 'Snowman,' hollering that there's a pileup about two miles ahead, right around a blind curve. Four four-wheelers and a flatbed all tangled up. If I hadn't had that radio, I would have plowed right into them. I was able to gear down, hit the jake brake—the engine retarder, you know, saves your service brakes from burning up on ice—and ease it onto the shoulder before I even saw the taillights. Now I always make sure my SWR meter is calibrated so my signal gets out strong. You gotta check your coax cables every few months too, make sure they aren't pinching in the door frame. \n\nBut, uh... eating less if I wasn't stressed? Yeah, I really don't know, doc. I just don't have a good answer for you on that one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I thought we just... didn't I just say I really couldn't tell you? I mean, I don't know if finding other ways to handle stress would make me eat less. I just don't have a clue about that, honestly. 

But, you know, speaking of handling things... let me tell you about trying to handle a rig when the wind kicks up across the Texas panhandle. You want to talk about stress? Try keeping a 53-foot box in your lane when you've got 50-mile-an-hour crosswinds hitting you broadside. It's like driving a giant sail. You're steering into the wind the whole time just to go straight. I was hauling a load of empty plastic bottles down I-40 near Amarillo last fall—worst kind of load for wind, 'cause it's so light. You don't have the weight holding you down. I saw a guy in a high-roof sleeper cab ahead of me get blown clean over onto his side. Just lifted the trailer right up and over he went. I had to pull off at the next Flying J and sit for fourteen hours waiting for the wind advisory to lift. You spend that much time in a truck stop, you start noticing all the little things, like how the lot lizards don't even bother coming out in weather like that, and how the coffee always tastes like it's been sitting on the burner since Tuesday. I spent half the time just polishing my aluminum wheels. You get a good metal polish and a buffer wheel on a cordless drill, and you can make those rims shine like a mirror. 

But yeah, doc, about the eating less thing... I really just don't know. I can't give you a straight answer on that because I just haven't tried it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc... it's kind of hard to say out loud. But what brings me in is... it's what happens after I do it. I’ll just be sitting there, usually in the cab of my truck, looking at all the empty containers and wrappers piled up, and I just feel completely disgusted with myself. I mean, I feel like I’m not the steady, in-control man I’ve always tried to be. \n\nAnd you really gotta be in control when you're hauling eighty-thousand pounds down the interstate, you know? I've been driving routes for almost thirty years. I actually got my associate's degree a long time ago thinking I'd go into logistics, maybe sit in an air-conditioned office somewhere. My buddy Tom did that, ended up with a nice desk job over in Toledo, but he absolutely hates it. Says the fluorescent lights give him terrible migraines, and his boss is a real piece of work. I always preferred the open road, just me and the radio, seeing the country... but anyway, yeah. I just sit there staring at the empty food containers and I don't even recognize the guy who did it.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you've done, before feeling disgusted, that's causing you distress?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's the eating. Like, some evenings I'll be at home, right? And I'll heat up what I swear is just one bowl of pozole. My sister makes it, she brings over these big batches. She’s always been the cook in the family, ever since our mom passed. Mom used to make this amazing green chili, and she’d simmer it all day. You could smell it from down the street. My sister tries, but it’s never quite the same, you know? She uses too much cumin, I think. Anyway, I'll start with just one bowl of the pozole, and before I know it, I’ve gone through the whole pot. And then a couple of tamales, a whole bag of chips, and whatever other leftovers are sitting in the fridge. It's like I'm feeding a whole crew instead of just myself. And it's not even like I'm tasting it after a while. I just... I just keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed or upset, or can it happen at any time?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I haven't really paid attention to whether I'm upset or if it just... happens whenever. I mean, stress is just kind of a constant, right? Especially on the road. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was taking a load down through Atlanta, and the traffic there is just an absolute nightmare. They’ve been doing construction on I-285 for what feels like a decade now. You’d think with all the highway taxes they collect, they could finish a simple repaving job on schedule. I was talking to my dispatcher, Jerry, about it on the radio—Jerry’s a decent guy, but he’s always breathing down your neck about delivery windows—and he was telling me his brother-in-law runs a paving company down in Georgia. Apparently, the cost of asphalt has gone through the roof lately. Something about the supply chains being all messed up still. It's crazy how everything is connected like that. \n\nBut... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about being stressed out when I eat. Yeah, I don't really know. I just haven't noticed if there's a pattern to it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating this way helps you feel better when you're dealing with stressful situations like the traffic in Atlanta?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it makes me feel better. I guess I haven't thought about it like that. I mean, when I'm stuck in that Atlanta traffic, the only thing that really makes me feel better is finally seeing the city limits in my rearview mirror. \n\nYou know, speaking of rearview mirrors, I had to replace the passenger side one on my rig a couple of weeks ago. Some guy in a rented box truck—you know the ones, where they just let anybody with a valid license drive a massive vehicle with zero training—he clipped me at a truck stop outside of Nashville. Didn't even stop. I had to call it in, wait for the company mechanic to authorize a replacement... it was a whole ordeal. And those mirrors aren't cheap, let me tell you. They've got the heating elements in them for the winter, the motorized adjustment... it's a piece of engineering. My buddy Tom, the one in Toledo, he was telling me about how they're putting cameras instead of mirrors on some of the new European trucks. Says it cuts down on aerodynamic drag. But I don't know, I just don't trust a screen the way I trust a piece of glass, you know? What if the camera glitches out when you're merging? \n\nBut, uh... I don't know if the eating helps with the stress. I just haven't really noticed if it does or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat this way more often when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: More often? I... I really couldn't say for sure if the frequency goes up. Like I said, the stress is just sort of always there in the background. \n\nYou know what really gets the blood pressure going, though? Those random DOT inspections. Man, you'll be rolling through Ohio, just trying to make good time, and the signs light up telling you to pull into the weigh station. You just pray they wave you through the bypass lane, but last month, this officer—young guy, probably fresh out of training and looking to prove something—decides he wants to do a full Level 1 inspection on my rig. Crawling under the trailer, checking the pushrod travel on the air brakes, going through my electronic logs with a fine-tooth comb. I keep my truck in top shape, always have, but they can always find *something* to write up if they look hard enough, you know? A marker light flickering, a mudflap half an inch too high. It took almost two hours, and by the time he finally handed me the paperwork, my whole schedule for the day was completely shot. I had to call the receiver, reschedule the dock time... it's a whole domino effect. The supply chain doesn't wait for anybody. \n\nBut, uh... gosh, I'm sorry. Did you ask if I eat more often when stuff like that happens? I honestly haven't kept track. I just don't know if there's a connection there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat this way as a way to deal with stress?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I really don't know. I haven't thought about it as a way to deal with things. It's just... I just eat. \n\nDealing with stress, though... my buddy Carl, he used to drive flatbeds out of Texas, he had this whole system for dealing with stress. He'd listen to these audiobooks on tape—well, CDs back then, I guess it's all on your phone now. But he’d listen to these crazy long biographies of presidents. Said it put his own problems in perspective. Like, he'd be stuck in a snowstorm in Wyoming, and he'd be listening to Abraham Lincoln dealing with the Civil War, and suddenly being delayed a day didn't seem so bad. Carl actually retired last year. Bought a little piece of land up in the panhandle, got a couple of horses. He always said he wanted to get away from the highways, and he finally did it. Good for him, you know? It takes a toll on your body, sitting in that seat for thirty years. Your lower back just goes. I've got this special lumbar support cushion I bought at a truck stop in Iowa, memory foam, cost me sixty bucks, but it's worth every penny. \n\nBut, uh... dealing with stress by eating? I honestly couldn't tell you. I just haven't noticed if that's why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, doc, I just don't know. Calming down? Feeling better? It's hard to say if the food actually does that. I've never really paid attention to whether I feel calmer after. \n\nSpeaking of calming down, though, you ever try to calm down a dispatcher when a load of refrigerated produce gets held up? Now that's a situation. I had this load of strawberries coming out of California a few years back. The reefer unit—the cooling system on the trailer—started acting up somewhere in New Mexico. The temperature gauge kept fluctuating, and with berries, you've got a really narrow window before they turn to mush. I'm on the phone with dispatch, and this guy, Kevin, he's practically hyperventilating on the other end of the line. \"Get it to a shop! Don't let the load spoil!\" Like I'm doing it on purpose, right? I had to pull into this tiny little repair place in Gallup. The mechanic there, old guy named Hector, he took one look at it, tapped a valve with a wrench, and the compressor kicked right back on. Charged me twenty bucks. Kevin was so relieved he actually authorized a steak dinner on the company card for that night. Best ribeye I ever had, right there at a diner off I-40. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... does eating calm me down? I really couldn't give you a straight answer on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: More when I'm anxious? I... I really don't think I could say. I just haven't kept track of it like that. I don't know if there's a pattern with the anxiety.\n\nYou know what makes me anxious, though? Driving through Atlanta during rush hour. I swear, they must hand out driver's licenses in cereal boxes down there. It's like a free-for-all. You've got these little sports cars darting in and out of the blind spots on a 70-foot rig, cutting across three lanes of traffic just to make an exit at the last possible second. My buddy Steve, he actually had a guy clip his front bumper doing that down near Macon. The guy spun out, went into the median, whole big mess. Steve had a dashcam running, thank God, or the insurance company would've tried to pin it on him. They always try to blame the trucker. I bought one of those dashcams myself right after that. Got it off Amazon, dual-lens, records the cab and the road. The memory card loop-records, so you don't even have to mess with it unless something happens. Peace of mind, you know? \n\nBut, uh... eating when I'm anxious? I just... I really don't know if I eat more then. I couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with stressful situations?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know, doc. Coping with stress? I just haven't really put two and two together on that. I don't know if it helps me cope or not.\n\nSpeaking of coping with situations, though... you ever try to find a parking spot at a rest stop on the I-95 corridor after 9 PM? Now that's stressful. You're running out of hours on your electronic log—the ELD, you know, it tracks every minute you're driving, and if you go over your 11 hours, you get a violation. So you're sweating, looking at the clock ticking down, pulling into these service plazas in Maryland or Delaware, and it's just wall-to-wall trucks. Guys are parked on the shoulders, on the off-ramps, making up spots that aren't even spots. I remember this one night, it was pouring rain, I had maybe fifteen minutes left on my clock, and I finally squeezed into this dirt lot behind a diner in Virginia. Woke up the next morning and my drive tires were sunk three inches deep in mud. Took me an hour of rocking the rig back and forth just to get out of there. Ruined a perfectly good pair of work boots, too. I had to buy a new pair at a Tractor Supply in Richmond. Good boots, though, steel-toed, waterproof. You need good arch support when you're jumping in and out of the cab all day. \n\nBut... using food to cope? I honestly don't know. I just haven't noticed if that's what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something you do to try and make yourself feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... well, making myself feel better? I don't think I can give you a real answer on that either. I just don't know if that's why I do it. I haven't really thought about it that way.\n\nYou want to talk about trying to make yourself feel better, though, you should try sleeping in a sleeper berth when it's 10 degrees outside and the APU goes down. The auxiliary power unit, it's what keeps the heat running without idling the main engine. I was up in North Dakota, near Fargo, middle of January. Woke up at 2 AM and I could see my breath in the cab. The APU had just quit. Turns out the fuel line to it had gelled up because of the cold. I had to bundle up in every blanket I had, plus my heavy winter coat, just shivering until the sun came up and I could get the main engine started to warm the place up. I carry a bottle of diesel 911 now, anti-gel additive, you know? Pour a little in the tanks when it gets that cold. Saves you a lot of grief. I get it by the gallon at the truck stops. \n\nBut, uh... eating to feel better? I really don't know, doc. I just haven't paid enough attention to tell you yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something that happens when you're trying to calm down after a stressful event?\n\nPatient: Calming down? I... I really couldn't say for sure. I don't know if I reach for food specifically to calm down. I just haven't connected those dots, honestly.\n\nBut if you want to talk about trying to calm down after something stressful... man, let me tell you about this blowout I had on I-80 out in Nebraska last summer. I'm hauling 40,000 pounds of frozen chicken, right? Just cruising along, maybe 65 miles an hour, and suddenly—BAM. Sounded like a shotgun went off right under my seat. My front right steer tire just shredded. The whole rig jerked hard toward the ditch. I had to wrestle that steering wheel with everything I had just to keep it on the pavement and ease it onto the shoulder without flipping the trailer. My heart was beating so hard I thought it was going to crack my ribs. I sat there on the side of the highway for a good twenty minutes, hands just shaking on the wheel, trying to get my breathing back to normal. Took road service three hours to get out there with a new tire. Cost the company a fortune, but hey, at least I didn't roll it. You really appreciate a good heavy-duty jack after watching a guy lift a fully loaded rig on the side of a busy interstate. \n\nBut as for the eating to calm down thing... I don't know, doc. I really just haven't noticed if that's what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this way if you weren't feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't stressed? I... I honestly couldn't tell you, doc. I don't even know what it looks like to not be stressed out, so I really don't know if my eating would change or not. I just haven't experienced it to know. \n\nI mean, speaking of not being stressed, the only time I ever really feel relaxed out on the road is when I'm bobtailing. That's when you're just driving the tractor, no trailer attached. It's a whole different ride. The suspension is a little stiff because it's meant to hold up tens of thousands of pounds, but man, the truck feels like a sports car compared to hauling a fully loaded 53-foot reefer. You can actually accelerate up a hill. But even then, your relaxation doesn't last long because you always gotta worry about the DOT. \n\nDepartment of Transportation, you know? You roll into a weigh station and they hit the bypass light and pull you around back for a Level 1 inspection. They're checking your slack adjusters, your airlines, crawling all under the rig on a creeper. I had this one inspector in Ohio last spring, guy spent forty-five minutes checking every single lug nut. Wrote me up because one of my amber clearance lights on the cab roof was flickering. Just flickering, not even completely burnt out! Cost the company a fine and hit my safety score. You gotta carry spare bulbs for every single socket on that truck, I swear. I keep a whole plastic tackle box full of fuses, headlights, and marker bulbs in my side box now. \n\nBut, uh... what were we saying? Eating when I'm not stressed? Yeah, I really don't have an answer for you on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\nPatient: I... well, if I found other ways to deal with stress? I really don't know. I can't say for sure if it would make a difference or not. I just haven't really tried anything else, so I couldn't tell you if I'd eat less.\n\nBut talking about dealing with stress... you know what really helps when you're out on the road? A good CB radio. A lot of the younger guys don't even use them anymore, they just rely on their phones and GPS, but let me tell you, a CB is a lifesaver. I run a Cobra 29 LX, peaked and tuned, with dual fiberglass whips mounted on the mirrors. You get out in Wyoming or Montana, where the cell service just drops off the face of the earth, and that radio is your only connection to the world. A few months ago, I was heading west on I-90, just past Billings, and the snow was coming down so thick you couldn't see the hood ornament. Total whiteout. But I had the radio on channel 19, and I hear this guy, 'Snowman,' hollering that there's a pileup about two miles ahead, right around a blind curve. Four four-wheelers and a flatbed all tangled up. If I hadn't had that radio, I would have plowed right into them. I was able to gear down, hit the jake brake—the engine retarder, you know, saves your service brakes from burning up on ice—and ease it onto the shoulder before I even saw the taillights. Now I always make sure my SWR meter is calibrated so my signal gets out strong. You gotta check your coax cables every few months too, make sure they aren't pinching in the door frame. \n\nBut, uh... eating less if I wasn't stressed? Yeah, I really don't know, doc. I just don't have a good answer for you on that one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\nPatient: Well, I thought we just... didn't I just say I really couldn't tell you? I mean, I don't know if finding other ways to handle stress would make me eat less. I just don't have a clue about that, honestly. \n\nBut, you know, speaking of handling things... let me tell you about trying to handle a rig when the wind kicks up across the Texas panhandle. You want to talk about stress? Try keeping a 53-foot box in your lane when you've got 50-mile-an-hour crosswinds hitting you broadside. It's like driving a giant sail. You're steering into the wind the whole time just to go straight. I was hauling a load of empty plastic bottles down I-40 near Amarillo last fall—worst kind of load for wind, 'cause it's so light. You don't have the weight holding you down. I saw a guy in a high-roof sleeper cab ahead of me get blown clean over onto his side. Just lifted the trailer right up and over he went. I had to pull off at the next Flying J and sit for fourteen hours waiting for the wind advisory to lift. You spend that much time in a truck stop, you start noticing all the little things, like how the lot lizards don't even bother coming out in weather like that, and how the coffee always tastes like it's been sitting on the burner since Tuesday. I spent half the time just polishing my aluminum wheels. You get a good metal polish and a buffer wheel on a cordless drill, and you can make those rims shine like a mirror. \n\nBut yeah, doc, about the eating less thing... I really just don't know. I can't give you a straight answer on that because I just haven't tried it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So you're sweating, looking at the clock ticking down, pulling into these service plazas in Maryland or Delaware, and it's just wall-to-wall trucks. Guys are parked on the shoulders, on the off-ramps, making up spots that aren't even spots. I remember this one night, it was pouring rain, I had maybe fifteen minutes left on my clock, and I finally squeezed into this dirt lot behind a diner in Virginia. Woke up the next morning and my drive tires were sunk three inches deep in mud. Took me an hour of rocking the rig back and forth just to get out of there. Ruined a perfectly good pair of work boots, too. I had to buy a new pair at a Tractor Supply in Richmond. Good boots, though, steel-toed, waterproof. You need good arch support when you're jumping in and out of the cab all day. \n\nBut... using food to cope? I honestly don't know. I just haven't noticed if that's what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something you do to try and make yourself feel better when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: I... well, making myself feel better? I don't think I can give you a real answer on that either. I just don't know if that's why I do it. I haven't really thought about it that way.\n\nYou want to talk about trying to make yourself feel better, though, you should try sleeping in a sleeper berth when it's 10 degrees outside and the APU goes down. The auxiliary power unit, it's what keeps the heat running without idling the main engine. I was up in North Dakota, near Fargo, middle of January. Woke up at 2 AM and I could see my breath in the cab. The APU had just quit. Turns out the fuel line to it had gelled up because of the cold. I had to bundle up in every blanket I had, plus my heavy winter coat, just shivering until the sun came up and I could get the main engine started to warm the place up. I carry a bottle of diesel 911 now, anti-gel additive, you know? Pour a little in the tanks when it gets that cold. Saves you a lot of grief. I get it by the gallon at the truck stops. \n\nBut, uh... eating to feel better? I really don't know, doc. I just haven't paid enough attention to tell you yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is something that happens when you're trying to calm down after a stressful event?\n\nPatient: Calming down? I... I really couldn't say for sure. I don't know if I reach for food specifically to calm down. I just haven't connected those dots, honestly.\n\nBut if you want to talk about trying to calm down after something stressful... man, let me tell you about this blowout I had on I-80 out in Nebraska last summer. I'm hauling 40,000 pounds of frozen chicken, right? Just cruising along, maybe 65 miles an hour, and suddenly—BAM. Sounded like a shotgun went off right under my seat. My front right steer tire just shredded. The whole rig jerked hard toward the ditch. I had to wrestle that steering wheel with everything I had just to keep it on the pavement and ease it onto the shoulder without flipping the trailer. My heart was beating so hard I thought it was going to crack my ribs. I sat there on the side of the highway for a good twenty minutes, hands just shaking on the wheel, trying to get my breathing back to normal. Took road service three hours to get out there with a new tire. Cost the company a fortune, but hey, at least I didn't roll it. You really appreciate a good heavy-duty jack after watching a guy lift a fully loaded rig on the side of a busy interstate. \n\nBut as for the eating to calm down thing... I don't know, doc. I really just haven't noticed if that's what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this way if you weren't feeling stressed?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't stressed? I... I honestly couldn't tell you, doc. I don't even know what it looks like to not be stressed out, so I really don't know if my eating would change or not. I just haven't experienced it to know. \n\nI mean, speaking of not being stressed, the only time I ever really feel relaxed out on the road is when I'm bobtailing. That's when you're just driving the tractor, no trailer attached. It's a whole different ride. The suspension is a little stiff because it's meant to hold up tens of thousands of pounds, but man, the truck feels like a sports car compared to hauling a fully loaded 53-foot reefer. You can actually accelerate up a hill. But even then, your relaxation doesn't last long because you always gotta worry about the DOT. \n\nDepartment of Transportation, you know? You roll into a weigh station and they hit the bypass light and pull you around back for a Level 1 inspection. They're checking your slack adjusters, your airlines, crawling all under the rig on a creeper. I had this one inspector in Ohio last spring, guy spent forty-five minutes checking every single lug nut. Wrote me up because one of my amber clearance lights on the cab roof was flickering. Just flickering, not even completely burnt out! Cost the company a fine and hit my safety score. You gotta carry spare bulbs for every single socket on that truck, I swear. I keep a whole plastic tackle box full of fuses, headlights, and marker bulbs in my side box now. \n\nBut, uh... what were we saying? Eating when I'm not stressed? Yeah, I really don't have an answer for you on that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\nPatient: I... well, if I found other ways to deal with stress? I really don't know. I can't say for sure if it would make a difference or not. I just haven't really tried anything else, so I couldn't tell you if I'd eat less.\n\nBut talking about dealing with stress... you know what really helps when you're out on the road? A good CB radio. A lot of the younger guys don't even use them anymore, they just rely on their phones and GPS, but let me tell you, a CB is a lifesaver. I run a Cobra 29 LX, peaked and tuned, with dual fiberglass whips mounted on the mirrors. You get out in Wyoming or Montana, where the cell service just drops off the face of the earth, and that radio is your only connection to the world. A few months ago, I was heading west on I-90, just past Billings, and the snow was coming down so thick you couldn't see the hood ornament. Total whiteout. But I had the radio on channel 19, and I hear this guy, 'Snowman,' hollering that there's a pileup about two miles ahead, right around a blind curve. Four four-wheelers and a flatbed all tangled up. If I hadn't had that radio, I would have plowed right into them. I was able to gear down, hit the jake brake—the engine retarder, you know, saves your service brakes from burning up on ice—and ease it onto the shoulder before I even saw the taillights. Now I always make sure my SWR meter is calibrated so my signal gets out strong. You gotta check your coax cables every few months too, make sure they aren't pinching in the door frame. \n\nBut, uh... eating less if I wasn't stressed? Yeah, I really don't know, doc. I just don't have a good answer for you on that one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat less if you found other ways to deal with your stress?\n\nPatient: Well, I thought we just... didn't I just say I really couldn't tell you? I mean, I don't know if finding other ways to handle stress would make me eat less. I just don't have a clue about that, honestly. \n\nBut, you know, speaking of handling things... let me tell you about trying to handle a rig when the wind kicks up across the Texas panhandle. You want to talk about stress? Try keeping a 53-foot box in your lane when you've got 50-mile-an-hour crosswinds hitting you broadside. It's like driving a giant sail. You're steering into the wind the whole time just to go straight. I was hauling a load of empty plastic bottles down I-40 near Amarillo last fall—worst kind of load for wind, 'cause it's so light. You don't have the weight holding you down. I saw a guy in a high-roof sleeper cab ahead of me get blown clean over onto his side. Just lifted the trailer right up and over he went. I had to pull off at the next Flying J and sit for fourteen hours waiting for the wind advisory to lift. You spend that much time in a truck stop, you start noticing all the little things, like how the lot lizards don't even bother coming out in weather like that, and how the coffee always tastes like it's been sitting on the burner since Tuesday. I spent half the time just polishing my aluminum wheels. You get a good metal polish and a buffer wheel on a cordless drill, and you can make those rims shine like a mirror. \n\nBut yeah, doc, about the eating less thing... I really just don't know. I can't give you a straight answer on that because I just haven't tried it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, are we... are we going in circles here? I feel like a broken record, but I really, truly do not know. I can't tell you if finding some other way to relax would stop me from eating so much. I just don't have an answer for that.

It's kind of like when my dispatcher, Gary, keeps asking me for an ETA when I'm stuck in dead-stop traffic on the Cross Bronx Expressway. I mean, what does he want me to say? "Gary, I'll be there when the sea of brake lights parts like Moses!" You know? You sit there creeping along at two miles an hour, watching the engine temp gauge creep up because there's no airflow through the radiator. That's when you really appreciate a good APU—an auxiliary power unit. A lot of the mega-carriers won't spec their trucks with them because of the upfront cost, but if you're an owner-operator or driving for a decent outfit, it's a must. You can shut the main engine off, save fuel, and the APU keeps your cab AC running and your batteries charged. It runs off a little two-cylinder diesel engine tucked behind the fairings. I had mine serviced in Omaha last month, needed a new alternator belt. The mechanic there, old guy named Earl, told me he used to work on Sherman tanks in the army. He showed me this trick with a pry bar to get the tension just right on the pulley without snapping the bracket...

But anyway, yeah, the stress and the eating. Like I said, I just don't know if anything would change. I really don't.
